Here, we\ne x a m i n et h em o r er e s t r i c t i v es e t t i n gw h e r er e t r o g r a d ec o m m u n i c a t i o nf r o m\nneuronal outputs is unavailable for pre-synaptic weight optimisation. We\npropose Forward Projection(FP), a randomised closed-form training method\nrequiring only a single forward pass over the dataset without retrograde\ncommunication. FP generates target values for pre-activation membrane\npotentials through randomised nonlinear projections of pre-synaptic inputs\nand labels. Local loss functions are optimised using closed-form regression\nwithout feedback from downstream layers. A key advantage is interpretability:\nmembrane potentials in FP-trained networks encode information inter-\npretable layer-wise as label predictions. Across several biomedical datasets, FP\nachieves generalisation comparable to gradient descent-based local learning\nmethods while requiring only a single forward propagation step, yielding\nsigniﬁcant training speedup. In few-shot learning tasks, FP produces more\ngeneralisable models than backpropagation-optimised alternatives, with local\ninterpretation functions successfully identifying clinically salient diagnostic\nfeatures.\nA core task in neural network training is synaptic “credit assignment”\nfor error in downstream layers1–3. Although backpropagation has been\nestablished as the standard approach to this problem, its biological\nplausibility has been questioned\n3–5. Backpropagation requires bidir-\nectional synaptic communication, which is incompatible with the\nunidirectional transmission of neural action potentials3. Consequently,\nbackpropagation would require either symmetric neural connectivity\nor a parallel retrograde network for error feedback to earlier layers\n3,6,7.\nThe backward pass traverses layers in reverse order of activation,\nleading to temporal discordance between forward and backward\noperations and necessitating storage of hidden activations. Further-\nmore, gradient descent requires hidden neural activations to be dif-\nferentiable throughout. To approximate backpropagation in a more\nbiologically plausible way, approaches such as Predictive Coding\n5,8 and\nDifference Target Propagation1 seek to align forward neural activity\nwith a backward network that mirrors the forward architecture,\neffectively implementing an inverse model. Various strategies have\nbeen proposed to address the credit assignment problem with\nreduced retrograde communication requirements. Auxiliary loss\nfunctions computed on the activations of individual layers have been\nproposed to shorten the backward pass\n9–12. A prominent example is the\nlayer-wise greedy optimisation method that aims to minimise the costs\nof backpropagation by applying local supervision (LS) at each layer9,13.\nExtensions of this approach include deep continuous local learning10\nand single-layer updating14. Hebbian learning rules using asymmetric\nfeedback weights have been explored, allowing for independent\nupdates for forward and feedback pathways\n15.\nRecent approaches have also explored using two forward passes\nto facilitate communication between upstream and downstream\nneurons\n16–19.T h e “Forward-Forward” (FF) learning algorithm 16 is an\napproach in which data and label hypotheses are combined as inputs,\nwith optimisation seeking to upregulate neural response to correctly\nlabelled inputs and subdue responses to spuriously labelled inputs.\nHowever, inference under the original Forward-Forward algorithm\nReceived: 14 January 2025\nAccepted: 26 January 2026\nCheck for updates\n1Centre for Intelligent Information Processing Systems, Department of Engineering, King’s College London, London, UK. 2Present address: Institute for\nIntelligent Networked Systems, Northeastern University London, E1 8PH London, United Kingdom. e-mail: k1930297@kcl.ac.uk; bipin.rajendran@kcl.ac.uk\nNature Communications|         (2026) 17:2414 1\n1234567890():,;\n1234567890():,;\n\n===== PAGE 2 =====\nrequires a forward pass for each hypothesised label, presenting issues\nfor tasks with large label spaces19.T h e“PEPITA\" algorithm employed a\npreliminary forward pass to predict a label, which is used to generate\nspurious data-label instances for training\n17.\nThe central issue with local learning methods in deep neural\nnetworks is that the optimal activity of hidden neurons is unknown\nduring training, preventing direct observation of local error. Pre-\ndeﬁning activity targets for hidden neurons allows for heuristic local\noptimisation. However, target de ﬁnition strategies are a topic of\nongoing research\n20. Techniques such as local-representation align-\nment and target propagation introduce target values for hidden acti-\nvations using limited retrograde communication from downstream\nneurons\n1,11,20–22. Alternatively, target activities can be set as ﬁxed ran-\ndom label projections computed during the forward pass, thereby\npermitting direct measurement of the error during the forward pass\n11.\nHowever, this approach may lead to highly correlated neuronal activity\n– a problem known as informational collapse 13. Although additive\nnoise permits maximal decorrelation of target potentials 1, it is unin-\nformative with respect to the label, potentially impeding modelﬁtting.\nApproaches such as random neural network features 23 forgo optimi-\nsation of hidden nodes entirely, instead projecting inputs to a random\nhigh-dimensional non-linear feature space. However, random feature\nlayers require exponential scaling with respect to the input dimension\nto support downstream learning\n24.\nThe “Predictive Coding ” paradigm provides a perspective on\nthe collective functionality of intermediate neurons, proposing that\nlocal neural learning processes optimise the prediction of pre-\nsynaptic neural inputs, minimising the “surprise” of out-of-\ndistribution stimuli 5. Approaches such as the “Difference Target\nPropagation”1 reframe network layers as a series of autoencoders,\nwhere intermediate activations represent a series of encodings,\ntransitioning from input information to label information. The issue\nof informational collapse in locally supervised SGD-trained models\nhas been addressed by optimising the retention of information on\nthe pre-synaptic activity\n13. “Prospective con ﬁguration” of target\nactivities reformulates learning under the presumption that idea-\nlised adjustments to neuronal activity should be generated via\nenergy minimisation before synaptic adjustment\n20. Synaptic weights\nmay be modi ﬁed to realise the predetermined activities in response\nto the given input stimuli 20, thereby transitioning from input infor-\nmation to label information through model layers. “Local neural\nsynchronisation\" generates target neural activity for hidden layers\nby projecting neuronal activity onto periodic basis vectors repre-\nsentative of class labels\n25.\nGoing beyond these approaches, we propose the use of random\nnonlinear projections of both pre-synaptic inputs and target labels\nto generate local target activities in the forward pass. The objective\nof this approach is to develop a neural network training algorithm\nthat requires no retrograde communication. Closed-form regression\ntechniques are applicable in this setting, permitting single-step layer\nweight computation without error feedback. Thus, weight para-\nmeters are determined without backward communication from\nneuronal outputs or downstream layers. Further advantages of this\nmethod include the direct interpretability of hidden neurons with\nrespect to local label predictions and stability in the few-shot\nsetting.\nResults\nWe consider a datasetD = fðxi, yiÞgN\ni =1 from the joint distribution (X , Y ).\nThe task is to learn a feed-forward neural network function mapping\nX ! Y .T h em o d e lh a sL layers, with dimensions m0, …, mL,a n da c t i -\nvations a0, …, aL,w h e r ea0 = x and aL = y. Each layer is equipped with\nweightsWl to generate membrane potentialszl = al−1Wl,a n da c t i v a t i o n\nfunctionf l : R ! R to generate neuronal outputsal = fl(zl). The model\nprediction is deﬁned as by = f LðWLð... f 1ðW1xÞÞÞ\n/C1\n.\nForward projection (FP)\nTo generate target membrane potentials for hidden neurons, we pre-\nsent the Forward Projection (FP) algorithm (Fig. 1A). We propose to\ncombine pre-synaptic inputs and labels using random non-linear pro-\njections to generate targets (Fig. 1B). For each training sample, the\ntarget potential ezl 2 R1× ml is generated from pre-synaptic inputs\nal/C0 1 2 R1× ml/C0 1 and labels y 2 R1× mL using ﬁxed random projection\nmatrices Ql 2 Rml/C0 1 × ml and Ul 2 RmL × ml such that\nezl = gl ðal/C0 1Ql Þ + gl ðyUl Þ, ð1Þ\nwhere gl : R ! R is an element-wise non-linear transformation. It is\nnoted here that gl is not necessarily equal to fl, the neural activation\nfunction. Ql and Ul are ﬁxed linear projections drawn from random\nGaussian distributions, which are pre-de ﬁned before training. The\ntarget potential ezl allows a local auxiliary loss function to be de ﬁned\nwith respect to the actual membrane potential,zl, realised during the\nforward pass. The randomised combination of pre-synaptic inputs\nwith the target label is inspired by the high-dimensional computing\nparadigm\n26,w h e r eﬁxed random projections are employed to encode\ninformation from multiple vector inputs, approximately preserving\nrelative distances according to the Johnson-Lindenstrauss lemma\n26.\nAccordingly, it is noted thatezl approximately encodes bothal−1 and y\n(see section S.8 in SI). By generating target potentials in a forward\nmanner, the mix of information encoded in each layer is expected to\ntransition incrementally from predominantly input information in\nearly layers to label information in later layers. We propose to deﬁne a\nlocal loss function L\nl : = k Zl /C0 eZl k, which may be employed as an\nobjective to optimise Wl. Synaptic weights for each layer can be\ncomputed in a closed-form forward manner, using ridge regression\n(Fig. 1C), such that\nW\nl : = ðA>\nl/C0 1Al/C0 1 + λIÞ\n/C0 1\nðA>\nl/C0 1\neZl Þ: ð2Þ\nHere, Al/C0 1 2 RN × ml/C0 1 and eZl 2 RN × ml are matrices of pre-synaptic\nactivities and target potentials, respectively, collected over the N\nsamples in D. λ is a regularisation term, and I is the identity matrix.\nObserve that the A>\nl/C0 1Al/C0 1 and A>\nl/C0 1\neZl terms in ( 2) may be computed\nsequentially over instances fa1, l/C0 1, ... , aN, l/C0 1g/C26 R1× ml/C0 1 ,a n d\nfez1, l , ... , ezN, l g/C26 R1× ml (see section S.7 in SI). Therefore, memory\nrequirements are independent of N, depending only on ml−1 and ml.\nNote, weights are computed once only, after the single training epoch.\nThe approach of encoding information on both inputs and labels in the\nhidden layers is inspired by the Predictive Coding and Target\nPropagation paradigms\n1,5,9. However, unlike previous approaches, no\nbackward communication is required from neuronal outputs to pre-\nsynaptic inputs to achieve the FP ﬁt. As a consequence of this ﬁtting\napproach, neural membrane potentials in hidden layers of FP-trained\nmodels may be interpreted as label predictions (Fig. 1C).\nForward projection performance\nForward Projection was compared to other local learning methods,\nincluding random features (RF) 24, Local Supervision (LS) 9,a n d\nForward-Forward (FF)16 in multiple tasks using equivalent model\narchitectures (Table 1). The Fashion MNIST (FMNIST) image classi ﬁ-\ncation task was modelled on a multi-layer perceptron (MLP). Forward\nProjection achieved higher test accuracy than other local learning\nmethods in this task, approaching the performance of the back-\npropagation reference standard. Two large-scale biomedical sequence\nmodelling tasks were also evaluated with one-dimensional convolu-\ntional neural network (1D-CNN) architectures. The Promoters task\nrequired the identiﬁcation of human non-TATA promoters, a class of\ngene promoter regions that increase transcription of DNA\nsequences\n27,28. Forward projection yielded higher test performance\nthan all other local learning methods in this task. The PTBXL-MI task29\nArticle https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-026-69161-1\nNature Communications|         (2026) 17:2414 2\n\n===== PAGE 3 =====\nrequired diagnosis of myocardial infarction (MI), a heart condition\ncommonly known as “heart attack\", from 12-channel electro-\ncardiogram (ECG) recordings. FP and LS performed comparably in this\ntask. FP also outperformed all other methods in the optimisation of\nmodels with more complex neuronal activations such as modulo and\npolynomial activation functions (see section S.14 in SI). Back-\npropagation performance benchmarks on the PTBXL-MI and Pro-\nmoters tasks were consistent with previous studies\n28,30. For the\nCIFAR10 classi ﬁcation task without data augmentation, two-\ndimensional convolutional neural networks (2D-CNNs) trained by FP\nunderﬁtted, but still outperformed all other local learning methods,\nwhereas standard backpropagation over ﬁtted the model. FP was\nimplemented in a transformer architecture to discriminate the ﬁrst\ntwo classes of the CIFAR10 dataset (\"CIFAR2\"), outperforming RF.\nAlternative feedback-free approaches.T oa s s e s st h ev a l u eo ft h eF P\ntarget generation function, we compared the performance of closed-\nform regression models ﬁtted to targets generated by alternative\nfunctions, including simple label projection ( eZ\nl : = yUl ) and label\nprojection with additive noise eZl : = yUl + E\n/C16/C17\n. To evaluate the cap-\nability of feedback-free training methods to handle information“bot-\ntlenecks\", they were applied to optimise MLP architectures for FMNIST\nclassiﬁcation. MLPs were generated with 1000 hidden neurons in the\nﬁrst and second layers (m\n1 = m2 = 1000), andm3 ∈ {100, 200, 400, 800}\nneurons in theﬁnal hidden layer. FP outperformed other feedback-free\napproaches (Fig. 2A). The performance of RF deteriorated in models\nwith small penultimate layers, as relevant information was less likely to\nbe represented by random projection\n24. The performance of simple\nlabel projection deteriorated in models with large penultimate layers, a\nresult that may be attributable to rank deﬁciency (see section S.6 in SI).\nNoisy label projection maintained steady performance, but at a lower\nlevel than FP.\nFew-shot learning\n\"Few-shot\" learning is a constrained learning scenario in which the\nnumber of data samples available for training is small. High-\ndimensional data, such as images, pose a challenge for few-shot\nl e a r n i n g ,a sm a n ys p u r i o u sf e a t u r e sm a ye x i s t .T h u s ,s u c c e s s f u lf e w -\nshot training methods must select generalisable features in the pre-\nsence of these confounders. Few-shot learning was assessed in image\nclassiﬁcation tasks using a 2D-CNN architecture. The optical coherence\ntomography task (OCT)\n31 required that the model discriminate images\nof healthy retinas from those with choroid neovascularization, a\npathology that affects the eye and manifests mainly as abnormal\ngrowth of blood vessels behind the retina. The paediatric chest X-ray\n(CXR) task\n31 required the model to discriminate between images of\nviral pneumonia, bacterial pneumonia, or healthy controls. The CIFAR2\ntask required the model to discriminate between theﬁrst two CIFAR10\nclasses (aeroplane and automobile). The training\nprocedure for each layer is presented in Supplementary Fig. S1.\nThe memory requirement for Forward Projection isOðm2Þ for the\nlayer weights,Oðm2Þ for the Q matrix, andOðmmLÞ for the U matrix. As\nthe A>\nl/C0 1Al/C0 1 and A>\nl/C0 1\neZl terms in (2) can be accumulated sequentially\nover data batches (See section S.7 in SI), twom × m matrices sufﬁce for\ntheir storage, thereby avoiding storage of theN × m matrices Al−1 and\neZl . As with all methods, Forward Projection requires Oðm2Þ multiply-\nand-accumulate (MAC) operations to calculate the activations for the\ndownstream layer in the forward pass. To generate target potentials\nduring the forward pass, Forward Projection also requires Oðm\n2Þ\nadditional MAC operations to project through Q and OðmmLÞ MAC\noperations to project through U. Computation of the A>\nl/C0 1Al/C0 1 and\nA>\nl/C0 1\neZl terms requires OðNm2Þ MAC operations each. After all samples\nhave been observed, computation of model weights using equation (2)\nrequires Oðm3Þ operations to invert the A>\nl/C0 1Al/C0 1 term and Oðm3Þ\noperations for matrix multiplication to complete the regression;\nhowever, this happens only once for each layer. Furthermore, Forward\nProjection requires no backward pass.\nFor backpropagation,Oðm\n2Þ memory is required for storing layer\nweights, Oðm2Þ for accumulated gradients, and OðmÞ for activations.\nFP\nLP\nLPN\nRF\n0.75\n0.78\n0.81\n0.84\n200 400 600 800\nHidden Layer Dimension\nTest Accuracy\nA\nBP\nFF\nFP\nLS\nRF\n0.5\n0.6\n0.7\n0.8\n10 20 30 40 50\nN Train\nTest AUC\nB\nBP\nFF\nFP\nLS\nRF\n0.5\n0.6\n0.7\n0.8\n0.9\n10 20 30 40 50\nN Train\nTest AUC\nC BP\nFF\nFP\nLS\nRF\n0.6\n0.7\n0.8\n0.9\n25 50 75 100\nN Train\nTest AUC\nD\nFig. 2 | Performance of Forward Projection with backpropagation and local\nlearning approaches. Here, we\n10\te x a m i n et h em o r er e s t r i c t i v es e t t i n gw h e r er e t r o g r a d ec o m m u n i c a t i o nf r o m\n11\tneuronal outputs is unavailable for pre-synaptic weight optimisation. We\n12\tpropose Forward Projection(FP), a randomised closed-form training method\n13\trequiring only a single forward pass over the dataset without retrograde\n14\tcommunication. FP generates target values for pre-activation membrane\n15\tpotentials through randomised nonlinear projections of pre-synaptic inputs\n16\tand labels. Local loss functions are optimised using closed-form regression\n17\twithout feedback from downstream layers. A key advantage is interpretability:\n18\tmembrane potentials in FP-trained networks encode information inter-\n19\tpretable layer-wise as label predictions. Across several biomedical datasets, FP\n20\tachieves generalisation comparable to gradient descent-based local learning\n21\tmethods while requiring only a single forward propagation step, yielding\n22\tsigniﬁcant training speedup. In few-shot learning tasks, FP produces more\n23\tgeneralisable models than backpropagation-optimised alternatives, with local\n24\tinterpretation functions successfully identifying clinically salient diagnostic\n25\tfeatures.\n26\tA core task in neural network training is synaptic “credit assignment”\n27\tfor error in downstream layers1–3. Although backpropagation has been\n28\testablished as the standard approach to this problem, its biological\n29\tplausibility has been questioned\n30\t3–5. Backpropagation requires bidir-\n31\tectional synaptic communication, which is incompatible with the\n32\tunidirectional transmission of neural action potentials3. Consequently,\n33\tbackpropagation would require either symmetric neural connectivity\n34\tor a parallel retrograde network for error feedback to earlier layers\n35\t3,6,7.\n36\tThe backward pass traverses layers in reverse order of activation,\n37\tleading to temporal discordance between forward and backward\n38\toperations and necessitating storage of hidden activations. Further-\n39\tmore, gradient descent requires hidden neural activations to be dif-\n40\tferentiable throughout. To approximate backpropagation in a more\n41\tbiologically plausible way, approaches such as Predictive Coding\n42\t5,8 and\n43\tDifference Target Propagation1 seek to align forward neural activity\n44\twith a backward network that mirrors the forward architecture,\n45\teffectively implementing an inverse model. Various strategies have\n46\tbeen proposed to address the credit assignment problem with\n47\treduced retrograde communication requirements. Auxiliary loss\n48\tfunctions computed on the activations of individual layers have been\n49\tproposed to shorten the backward pass\n50\t9–12. A prominent example is the\n51\tlayer-wise greedy optimisation method that aims to minimise the costs\n52\tof backpropagation by applying local supervision (LS) at each layer9,13.\n53\tExtensions of this approach include deep continuous local learning10\n54\tand single-layer updating14. Hebbian learning rules using asymmetric\n55\tfeedback weights have been explored, allowing for independent\n56\tupdates for forward and feedback pathways\n57\t15.\n58\tRecent approaches have also explored using two forward passes\n59\tto facilitate communication between upstream and downstream\n60\tneurons\n61\t16–19.T h e “Forward-Forward” (FF) learning algorithm 16 is an\n62\tapproach in which data and label hypotheses are combined as inputs,\n63\twith optimisation seeking to upregulate neural response to correctly\n64\tlabelled inputs and subdue responses to spuriously labelled inputs.\n65\tHowever, inference under the original Forward-Forward algorithm\n66\tReceived: 14 January 2025\n67\tAccepted: 26 January 2026\n68\tCheck for updates\n69\t1Centre for Intelligent Information Processing Systems, Department of Engineering, King’s College London, London, UK. 2Present address: Institute for\n70\tIntelligent Networked Systems, Northeastern University London, E1 8PH London, United Kingdom. e-mail: k1930297@kcl.ac.uk; bipin.rajendran@kcl.ac.uk\n71\tNature Communications|         (2026) 17:2414 1\n72\t1234567890():,;\n73\t1234567890():,;\n74\t\n75\t===== PAGE 2 =====\n76\trequires a forward pass for each hypothesised label, presenting issues\n77\tfor tasks with large label spaces19.T h e“PEPITA\" algorithm employed a\n78\tpreliminary forward pass to predict a label, which is used to generate\n79\tspurious data-label instances for training\n80\t17.\n81\tThe central issue with local learning methods in deep neural\n82\tnetworks is that the optimal activity of hidden neurons is unknown\n83\tduring training, preventing direct observation of local error. Pre-\n84\tdeﬁning activity targets for hidden neurons allows for heuristic local\n85\toptimisation. However, target de ﬁnition strategies are a topic of\n86\tongoing research\n87\t20. Techniques such as local-representation align-\n88\tment and target propagation introduce target values for hidden acti-\n89\tvations using limited retrograde communication from downstream\n90\tneurons\n91\t1,11,20–22. Alternatively, target activities can be set as ﬁxed ran-\n92\tdom label projections computed during the forward pass, thereby\n93\tpermitting direct measurement of the error during the forward pass\n94\t11.\n95\tHowever, this approach may lead to highly correlated neuronal activity\n96\t– a problem known as informational collapse 13. Although additive\n97\tnoise permits maximal decorrelation of target potentials 1, it is unin-\n98\tformative with respect to the label, potentially impeding modelﬁtting.\n99\tApproaches such as random neural network features 23 forgo optimi-\n100\tsation of hidden nodes entirely, instead projecting inputs to a random\n101\thigh-dimensional non-linear feature space. However, random feature\n102\tlayers require exponential scaling with respect to the input dimension\n103\tto support downstream learning\n104\t24.\n105\tThe “Predictive Coding ” paradigm provides a perspective on\n106\tthe collective functionality of intermediate neurons, proposing that\n107\tlocal neural learning processes optimise the prediction of pre-\n108\tsynaptic neural inputs, minimising the “surprise” of out-of-\n109\tdistribution stimuli 5. Approaches such as the “Difference Target\n110\tPropagation”1 reframe network layers as a series of autoencoders,\n111\twhere intermediate activations represent a series of encodings,\n112\ttransitioning from input information to label information. The issue\n113\tof informational collapse in locally supervised SGD-trained models\n114\thas been addressed by optimising the retention of information on\n115\tthe pre-synaptic activity\n116\t13. “Prospective con ﬁguration” of target\n117\tactivities reformulates learning under the presumption that idea-\n118\tlised adjustments to neuronal activity should be generated via\n119\tenergy minimisation before synaptic adjustment\n120\t20. Synaptic weights\n121\tmay be modi ﬁed to realise the predetermined activities in response\n122\tto the given input stimuli 20, thereby transitioning from input infor-\n123\tmation to label information through model layers. “Local neural\n124\tsynchronisation\" generates target neural activity for hidden layers\n125\tby projecting neuronal activity onto periodic basis vectors repre-\n126\tsentative of class labels\n127\t25.\n128\tGoing beyond these approaches, we propose the use of random\n129\tnonlinear projections of both pre-synaptic inputs and target labels\n130\tto generate local target activities in the forward pass. The objective\n131\tof this approach is to develop a neural network training algorithm\n132\tthat requires no retrograde communication. Closed-form regression\n133\ttechniques are applicable in this setting, permitting single-step layer\n134\tweight computation without error feedback. Thus, weight para-\n135\tmeters are determined without backward communication from\n136\tneuronal outputs or downstream layers. Further advantages of this\n137\tmethod include the direct interpretability of hidden neurons with\n138\trespect to local label predictions and stability in the few-shot\n139\tsetting.\n140\tResults\n141\tWe consider a datasetD = fðxi, yiÞgN\n142\ti =1 from the joint distribution (X , Y ).\n143\tThe task is to learn a feed-forward neural network function mapping\n144\tX ! Each layer is equipped with\n146\tweightsWl to generate membrane potentialszl = al−1Wl,a n da c t i v a t i o n\n147\tfunctionf l : R ! R to generate neuronal outputsal = fl(zl). The model\n148\tprediction is deﬁned as by = f LðWLð... f 1ðW1xÞÞÞ\n149\t/C1\n150\t.\n151\tForward projection (FP)\n152\tTo generate target membrane potentials for hidden neurons, we pre-\n153\tsent the Forward Projection (FP) algorithm (Fig. 1A). We propose to\n154\tcombine pre-synaptic inputs and labels using random non-linear pro-\n155\tjections to generate targets (Fig. 1B). For each training sample, the\n156\ttarget potential ezl 2 R1× ml is generated from pre-synaptic inputs\n157\tal/C0 1 2 R1× ml/C0 1 and labels y 2 R1× mL using ﬁxed random projection\n158\tmatrices Ql 2 Rml/C0 1 × ml and Ul 2 RmL × ml such that\n159\tezl = gl ðal/C0 1Ql Þ + gl ðyUl Þ, ð1Þ\n160\twhere gl : R ! R is an element-wise non-linear transformation. It is\n161\tnoted here that gl is not necessarily equal to fl, the neural activation\n162\tfunction. Ql and Ul are ﬁxed linear projections drawn from random\n163\tGaussian distributions, which are pre-de ﬁned before training. The\n164\ttarget potential ezl allows a local auxiliary loss function to be de ﬁned\n165\twith respect to the actual membrane potential,zl, realised during the\n166\tforward pass. The randomised combination of pre-synaptic inputs\n167\twith the target label is inspired by the high-dimensional computing\n168\tparadigm\n169\t26,w h e r eﬁxed random projections are employed to encode\n170\tinformation from multiple vector inputs, approximately preserving\n171\trelative distances according to the Johnson-Lindenstrauss lemma\n172\t26.\n173\tAccordingly, it is noted thatezl approximately encodes bothal−1 and y\n174\t(see section S.8 in SI). By generating target potentials in a forward\n175\tmanner, the mix of information encoded in each layer is expected to\n176\ttransition incrementally from predominantly input information in\n177\tearly layers to label information in later layers. We propose to deﬁne a\n178\tlocal loss function L\n179\tl : = k Zl /C0 eZl k, which may be employed as an\n180\tobjective to optimise Wl. Synaptic weights for each layer can be\n181\tcomputed in a closed-form forward manner, using ridge regression\n182\t(Fig. 1C), such that\n183\tW\n184\tl : = ðA>\n185\tl/C0 1Al/C0 1 + λIÞ\n186\t/C0 1\n187\tðA>\n188\tl/C0 1\n189\teZl Þ: ð2Þ\n190\tHere, Al/C0 1 2 RN × ml/C0 1 and eZl 2 RN × ml are matrices of pre-synaptic\n191\tactivities and target potentials, respectively, collected over the N\n192\tsamples in D. λ is a regularisation term, and I is the identity matrix.\n193\tObserve that the A>\n194\tl/C0 1Al/C0 1 and A>\n195\tl/C0 1\n196\teZl terms in ( 2) may be computed\n197\tsequentially over instances fa1, l/C0 1, ... , aN, l/C0 1g/C26 R1× ml/C0 1 ,a n d\n198\tfez1, l , ... , ezN, l g/C26 R1× ml (see section S.7 in SI). Therefore, memory\n199\trequirements are independent of N, depending only on ml−1 and ml.\n200\tNote, weights are computed once only, after the single training epoch.\n201\tThe approach of encoding information on both inputs and labels in the\n202\thidden layers is inspired by the Predictive Coding and Target\n203\tPropagation paradigms\n204\t1,5,9. However, unlike previous approaches, no\n205\tbackward communication is required from neuronal outputs to pre-\n206\tsynaptic inputs to achieve the FP ﬁt. As a consequence of this ﬁtting\n207\tapproach, neural membrane potentials in hidden layers of FP-trained\n208\tmodels may be interpreted as label predictions (Fig. 1C).\n209\tForward projection performance\n210\tForward Projection was compared to other local learning methods,\n211\tincluding random features (RF) 24, Local Supervision (LS) 9,a n d\n212\tForward-Forward (FF)16 in multiple tasks using equivalent model\n213\tarchitectures (Table 1). The Fashion MNIST (FMNIST) image classi ﬁ-\n214\tcation task was modelled on a multi-layer perceptron (MLP). Forward\n215\tProjection achieved higher test accuracy than other local learning\n216\tmethods in this task, approaching the performance of the back-\n217\tpropagation reference standard. Two large-scale biomedical sequence\n218\tmodelling tasks were also evaluated with one-dimensional convolu-\n219\ttional neural network (1D-CNN) architectures. The Promoters task\n220\trequired the identiﬁcation of human non-TATA promoters, a class of\n221\tgene promoter regions that increase transcription of DNA\n222\tsequences\n223\t27,28. Forward projection yielded higher test performance\n224\tthan all other local learning methods in this task. The PTBXL-MI task29\n225\tArticle https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-026-69161-1\n226\tNature Communications|         (2026) 17:2414 2\n227\t\n228\t===== PAGE 3 =====\n229\trequired diagnosis of myocardial infarction (MI), a heart condition\n230\tcommonly known as “heart attack\", from 12-channel electro-\n231\tcardiogram (ECG) recordings. FP and LS performed comparably in this\n232\ttask. FP also outperformed all other methods in the optimisation of\n233\tmodels with more complex neuronal activations such as modulo and\n234\tpolynomial activation functions (see section S.14 in SI). Back-\n235\tpropagation performance benchmarks on the PTBXL-MI and Pro-\n236\tmoters tasks were consistent with previous studies\n237\t28,30. For the\n238\tCIFAR10 classi ﬁcation task without data augmentation, two-\n239\tdimensional convolutional neural networks (2D-CNNs) trained by FP\n240\tunderﬁtted, but still outperformed all other local learning methods,\n241\twhereas standard backpropagation over ﬁtted the model. FP was\n242\timplemented in a transformer architecture to discriminate the ﬁrst\n243\ttwo classes of the CIFAR10 dataset (\"CIFAR2\"), outperforming RF.\n244\tAlternative feedback-free approaches.T oa s s e s st h ev a l u eo ft h eF P\n245\ttarget generation function, we compared the performance of closed-\n246\tform regression models ﬁtted to targets generated by alternative\n247\tfunctions, including simple label projection ( eZ\n248\tl : = yUl ) and label\n249\tprojection with additive noise eZl : = yUl + E\n250\t/C16/C17\n251\t. To evaluate the cap-\n252\tability of feedback-free training methods to handle information“bot-\n253\ttlenecks\", they were applied to optimise MLP architectures for FMNIST\n254\tclassiﬁcation. MLPs were generated with 1000 hidden neurons in the\n255\tﬁrst and second layers (m\n256\t1 = m2 = 1000), andm3 ∈ {100, 200, 400, 800}\n257\tneurons in theﬁnal hidden layer. FP outperformed other feedback-free\n258\tapproaches (Fig. 2A). The performance of RF deteriorated in models\n259\twith small penultimate layers, as relevant information was less likely to\n260\tbe represented by random projection\n261\t24. The performance of simple\n262\tlabel projection deteriorated in models with large penultimate layers, a\n263\tresult that may be attributable to rank deﬁciency (see section S.6 in SI).\n264\tNoisy label projection maintained steady performance, but at a lower\n265\tlevel than FP.\n266\tFew-shot learning\n267\t\"Few-shot\" learning is a constrained learning scenario in which the\n268\tnumber of data samples available for training is small. High-\n269\tdimensional data, such as images, pose a challenge for few-shot\n270\tl e a r n i n g ,a sm a n ys p u r i o u sf e a t u r e sm a ye x i s t .T h u s ,s u c c e s s f u lf e w -\n271\tshot training methods must select generalisable features in the pre-\n272\tsence of these confounders. Few-shot learning was assessed in image\n273\tclassiﬁcation tasks using a 2D-CNN architecture. The optical coherence\n274\ttomography task (OCT)\n275\t31 required that the model discriminate images\n276\tof healthy retinas from those with choroid neovascularization, a\n277\tpathology that affects the eye and manifests mainly as abnormal\n278\tgrowth of blood vessels behind the retina. The paediatric chest X-ray\n279\t(CXR) task\n280\t31 required the model to discriminate between images of\n281\tviral pneumonia, bacterial pneumonia, or healthy controls. The CIFAR2\n282\ttask required the model to discriminate between theﬁrst two CIFAR10\n283\tclasses (aeroplane and automobile). Few-shot performance is tabulated in Supplemental\n345\tTable S2.\n346\tFeature interpretability\n347\tExplainability is a central issue with backpropagation-based learning,\n348\tas relationships between hidden activations and model predictions\n349\tmay be non-monotonic, complicating the interpretation of hidden\n350\tneural activities\n351\t32. The training\n445\tprocedure for each layer is presented in Supplementary Fig. S1.\n446\tThe memory requirement for Forward Projection isOðm2Þ for the\n447\tlayer weights,Oðm2Þ for the Q matrix, andOðmmLÞ for the U matrix. As\n448\tthe A>\n449\tl/C0 1Al/C0 1 and A>\n450\tl/C0 1\n451\teZl terms in (2) can be accumulated sequentially\n452\tover data batches (See section S.7 in SI), twom × m matrices sufﬁce for\n453\ttheir storage, thereby avoiding storage of theN × m matrices Al−1 and\n454\teZl . As with all methods, Forward Projection requires Oðm2Þ multiply-\n455\tand-accumulate (MAC) operations to calculate the activations for the\n456\tdownstream layer in the forward pass. To generate target potentials\n457\tduring the forward pass, Forward Projection also requires Oðm\n458\t2Þ\n459\tadditional MAC operations to project through Q and OðmmLÞ MAC\n460\toperations to project through U. Computation of the A>\n461\tl/C0 1Al/C0 1 and\n462\tA>\n463\tl/C0 1\n464\teZl terms requires OðNm2Þ MAC operations each. After all samples\n465\thave been observed, computation of model weights using equation (2)\n466\trequires Oðm3Þ operations to invert the A>\n467\tl/C0 1Al/C0 1 term and Oðm3Þ\n468\toperations for matrix multiplication to complete the regression;\n469\thowever, this happens only once for each layer. Furthermore, Forward\n470\tProjection requires no backward pass.\n471\tFor backpropagation,Oðm\n472\t2Þ memory is required for storing layer\n473\tweights, Oðm2Þ for accumulated gradients, and OðmÞ for activations.\n474\tFP\n475\tLP\n476\tLPN\n477\tRF\n478\t0.75\n479\t0.78\n480\t0.81\n481\t0.84\n482\t200 400 600 800\n483\tHidden Layer Dimension\n484\tTest Accuracy\n485\tA\n486\tBP\n487\tFF\n488\tFP\n489\tLS\n490\tRF\n491\t0.5\n492\t0.6\n493\t0.7\n494\t0.8\n495\t10 20 30 40 50\n496\tN Train\n497\tTest AUC\n498\tB\n499\tBP\n500\tFF\n501\tFP\n502\tLS\n503\tRF\n504\t0.5\n505\t0.6\n506\t0.7\n507\t0.8\n508\t0.9\n509\t10 20 30 40 50\n510\tN Train\n511\tTest AUC\n512\tC BP\n513\tFF\n514\tFP\n515\tLS\n516\tRF\n517\t0.6\n518\t0.7\n519\t0.8\n520\t0.9\n521\t25 50 75 100\n522\tN Train\n523\tTest AUC\n524\tD\n525\tFig. 2 | Performance of Forward Projection with backpropagation and local\n526\tlearning approaches. Notably, compute scales as Oðm3Þ for FP versus\n548\tOðNem2Þ for BP. For example, training a dense hidden layer with 1000\n549\tinputs and 1000 outputs on the FMNIST dataset (N = 60, 000, mL =1 0 )\n550\tby Forward Projection requires a total of 1.2 × 1011 MAC operations for\n551\tthe forward pass and 1.2 × 1011 MAC operations for the weight update.\n552\tTraining the same layer via 100 epochs of backpropagation requires\n553\t6.0 × 10\n554\t12 MAC operations for forward passes and 1.8 × 1013 operations\n555\tfor weight updates. Forward projection requires only a single training\n556\tepoch, reducing overall computation time accordingly. Note that Local\n557\tSupervision and Forward-Forward algorithms have similar computa-\n558\ttional and memory requirements as backpropagation (Table 2 and\n559\tTable S3). We also present the wall-clock time for full training of an\n560\tFig. 3 | Forward Projection layer interpretations for electrocardiogram analy-\n561\tsis. Further timing\n589\tresults are provided in Supplementary Table S3. Computations were\n590\trun using the Google Colab service with an NVIDIA T4 graphics pro-\n591\tcessing unit. Training complexities of other methods are discussed in\n592\ts e c t i o nS . 1 3i nS I .\n593\tDiscussion\n594\tWe present the Forward Projection algorithm, which enables learning\n595\tin a single pass over the dataset using random projections and closed-\n596\tform optimisation. Compared to state-of-the-art local learning meth-\n597\tods that require observing post-synaptic neuronal outputs to optimise\n598\tsynaptic weights via error-based gradient descent, Forward Projection\n599\toperates under a stricter constraint,ﬁtting weights using just the pre-\n600\tsynaptic neuronal activity and labels.\n601\tThe target generation function proposed here for Forward Pro-\n602\tjection promotes input and label encoding in neural membrane\n603\tpotentials (see section S.8 in SI). Joint encoding of labels with pre-\n604\tsynaptic activity alleviates the degenerate neural activity which results\n605\tfrom local modelling of simple label projections, whilst avoiding the\n606\tintroduction of uninformative noise (see section S.6 in SI). In this\n607\tanalysis, a simple non-linearity was employed for target generation.\n608\tThe utility of more complex nonlinearities for target generation is a\n609\tsubject for further research.\n610\tExplainability of neural network models is an important limitation\n611\tin decision-critical ﬁelds such as biomedicine, where errors such as\n612\tconfounded decisions may lead to signi ﬁcant consequences\n613\t35.F P -\n614\ttrained layers may be interpreted without downstream information,\n615\tproviding insight into model reasoning in hidden layers. FP inter-\n616\tpretation yielded informative outputs in three model architectures,\n617\tidentifying clinically salient features in ECG sequences and OCT ima-\n618\tges. An important advantage of FP training is that saliency maps may\n619\tbe generated before downstream ﬁtting, permitting on-the- ﬂy\n620\tinspection of intermediate layers for suf ﬁciency or confounding.\n621\tThus, expert scrutiny of hidden layer performance may be conducted\n622\teven before the downstream architecture isﬁnalised.\n623\tFew-shot learning is ubiquitous in biological systems, which\n624\texhibit rapid neuronal adaptation to changing environments\n625\t36.I no u r\n626\texperiments, Forward Projection demonstrated clear performance\n627\tadvantages over backpropagation and local learning approaches in\n628\tfew-shot learning tasks, presenting a plausible method for learning\n629\tnew tasks rapidly. In this setting, convolutional features learned by\n630\tForward Projection yielded more generalisable models than back-\n631\tpropagation, which overﬁtted in some experiments and underﬁtted in\n632\tothers, even underperforming random features in some cases. For-\n633\tward Projection also maintained reasonable performance on activa-\n634\ttion functions that were untrainable by SGD-based methods (see\n635\ts e c t i o nS . 1 4i nS I ) .\n636\tThe closed-form Forward Projectionﬁti sc o m p u t a b l ei nas i n g l e\n637\tpass over the data for each layer, presenting an opportunity to\n638\texpedite training and reduce environmental footprint. The efﬁciency\n639\tof FP training is attributable to a substantially different operational\n640\tsequence from that employed in SGD-based approaches. Firstly, FP\n641\ttraining collects a Gram matrix of pre-synaptic activity over a single\n642\tepoch, which is subsequently inverted during a one-step weight matrix\n643\tcomputation. Secondly, FP completes ﬁtting for each layer before\n644\tinitialising successive layers. In contrast, SGD-based approachesﬁta l l\n645\tthe layers in the network iteratively, using feedback from neuronal\n646\toutputs and downstream layers. FP training requires no retrograde\n647\tcommunication between neural output activations and presynaptic\n648\tconnections, enabling direct training on hardware with unidirectional\n649\tsynaptic and neuronal communication. This differs from current\n650\titerative local learning methods, which require backward commu-\n651\tnication from neuronal outputs to optimise pre-synaptic parameters\n652\t(see section S.2 in SI). Although FP addresses the feedback-free con-\n653\tstraint inherent in biological learning systems, the biological plausi-\n654\tbility of one-step learning via matrix inversion remains uncertain.\n655\tIterative ﬁtting via gradient descent in sequential, batch-based learn-\n656\ting offers a practical and scalable approach for aligning weights with\n657\tlocally generated targets, as de ﬁned in ( 1) (see section S.11 in SI).\n658\tAlthough closed-form optimisation remains a challenge for recurrent\n659\tarchitectures, future research will explore integrating iterative training\n660\twith random temporal convolutions to generalise the FP framework to\n661\tdynamic models and expand its applicability to temporal learning\n662\ttasks. This backpropagation-free strategy holds promise for biologi-\n663\tcally inspired computing systems, such as spiking neural networks,\n664\twhere non-differentiable activation functions preclude standard\n665\tbackpropagation\n666\t37–39. FP training may be applicable as a pre-training\n667\tstep to reduce the number of training epochs required for back-\n668\tpropagation. In conclusion, FP is an ef ﬁcient approach for neural\n669\tnetwork optimisation, employing techniques from randomised pro-\n670\tjective embedding and linear regression to ﬁtw e i g h tm a t r i c e si no n e\n671\tepoch using a single-step solution. Interpretability of hidden neurons\n672\tin FP-trained models may be employed to improve the explainability of\n673\tneural network predictions.\n674\tMethods\n675\tGeneralisability of machine learning methods to real-world datasets\n676\trequires robustness to adverse modelling conditions such as class\n677\timbalance and noise, which often impede performance\n678\t40.T oa s s e s st h e\n679\tapplicability and generalisability of Forward Projection and local\n680\tlearning methods in diverse real-world conditions, performance was\n681\tevaluated in benchmark tasks from four biomedical domains\n682\tdescribed below.\n683\tPTBXL-MI\n684\tThe PTB-XL dataset contains 12-lead electrocardiography (ECG)\n685\trecordings from 18,889 participants\n686\t29,41. ECG recordings of ten-second\n687\tduration and 100Hz sample rate were used in our experiments. Data instances with uncertain diagnoses were excluded from\n709\tthis analysis. Following recommendations of the dataset authors who\n710\tprovided predeﬁned participant-disjoint dataset splits, the tenth fold\n711\twas held out for model testing\n712\t29.T h e ﬁrst 15 MI-positive ECGs in the\n713\ttest set were annotated by a medical doctor to identify diagnostically\n714\trelevant sections for quantitative explainability evaluation.\n715\tPromoters\n716\tThe Human Non-TATA Promoters dataset (\"Promoters\") was extracted\n717\tfrom the GenomicBenchmarks repository\n718\t28. Data was originally pub-\n719\tlished in ref. 27. 36,131 nucleotide sequences of 251 bases each were\n720\tanalysed. Nucleotide sequences were converted to 4-channel one-hot\n721\tvectors indicating adenine, cytosine, guanine and thymine. Inde-\n722\tterminate bases were represented with zero vectors. Models were\n723\trequired to classify the promoter functionality of the sequence as\n724\t“promoter\" ( N\n725\ttrain = 12,355; Ntest =4 1 1 9 )o r “non-promoter\"\n726\t(Ntrain =1 4 ,7 4 2 ;Ntest = 4915).\n727\tCXR\n728\tThe paediatric pneumonia chest X-ray dataset (\"CXR\") is a retro-\n729\tspective cohort of patients aged between one andﬁve years, recorded\n730\tin Guangzhou Women and Children ’s Medical Center, Guangzhou,\n731\toriginally published in ref. 31. Chest X-ray images were recorded as\n732\tpart of routine care during diagnostic workup for suspected lower\n733\trespiratory tract infection. During data collection, clinicians screened\n734\tthe images for quality and excluded those with severe artefacts or\n735\tcorruption. Images were annotated by two expert physicians. Local\n736\tinstitutional review board approvals were obtained. Images were loa-\n737\tded in greyscale, rescaled to the [0, 1] intensity range and resized to\n738\t128 × 128 pixels by bilinear interpolation. Models were required to\n739\tclassify images as “normal\" (N\n740\ttrain =1 3 4 9 ;Ntest = 234), “viral pneumo-\n741\tnia\" ( Ntrain =2 5 3 8 ; Ntest =2 4 2 ) ,o r “bacterial pneumonia\"\n742\t(Ntrain =1 3 4 5 ;Ntest =1 4 8 ) .\n743\tOCT\n744\tThe optical coherence tomography dataset (\"OCT\") is a retrospective\n745\tcohort of adult patients from ﬁve ophthalmology institutions in the\n746\tUSA and China recorded between 2013 and 2017 during routine care,\n747\toriginally published in ref.31. Images were initially annotated by local\n748\tmedical students, who had received OCT interpretation training.\n749\tSubsequent annotation was performed by four ophthalmologists and\n750\ttwo independent retinal specialists. Horizontal foveal cut images were\n751\tavailable in portable network graphics image format. Images were\n752\tloaded in greyscale, rescaled to the [0, 1] intensity range, and resized to\n753\t128 × 128 pixels by bilinear interpolation. Models were required to\n754\tclassify images as either“normal\" (N\n755\ttrain =2 9 2 6 ;Ntest =1 4 9 )o r“choroid\n756\tneovascularisation\" (Ntrain =7 9 1 ;Ntest =1 7 8 ) .\n757\tModel training\n758\tThe FMNIST dataset was modelled using an MLP with 3 × 1000 hidden\n759\tReLU-activated neurons. Sequential datasets (PTBXL-MI and Pro-\n760\tmoters) were modelled by a 1D-CNN architecture of four convolutional\n761\tblocks. Each convolutional block included two convolutional layers\n762\twith kernel dimensions 3 and strides of 1 and 2, respectively. Con-\n763\tvolutional layers in the l-th block had 32 × 2\n764\tl−1 ﬁlters. Convolutional\n765\timplementation is detailed in Supplementary section S.3. Convolu-\n766\ttional outputs were aggregated by global average pooling in the\n767\tpenultimate layer. For gradient-descent-based learning algorithms,\n768\tbatch normalisation layers were included between convolutional\n769\tblocks. CIFAR2 modelling in Table 1 used a vision transformer\n770\tarchitecture\n771\t42 operating on image patches of dimension 4 × 4, with a\n772\tsequential stack of four multi-headed attention layers, each having 8\n773\theads, embedding dimension 64, and MLP dimension 64. 2D-CNN\n774\tarchitectures had kernel dimension 3 × 3 and the l-th convolutional\n775\tblock had 16 × 2 l−1 ﬁlters (CXR and OCT), 32 × 2 l−1 ﬁlters (CIFAR2), or\n776\t64 × 2l−1 ﬁlters (CIFAR10). The sign function was employed to generate\n777\ttarget activations for Forward Projection models, such that\n778\tezl =s i g nðal/C0 1Ql Þ +s i g nðyUl Þ: ð4Þ\n779\tData augmentation was not performed in our experiments. Models\n780\twere ﬁtted using the PyTorch library. For SGD-based methods, early\n781\tstopping was performed according to validation loss. Model weight\n782\tinitialisation was random, and optimisation was performed with the\n783\tAdam optimiser with a learning rate of 0.001, training to minimise\n784\tvalidation loss by early stopping with a patience ofﬁve epochs. In few-\n785\tshot experiments, the learning rate was reduced to 0.0001 for SGD-\n786\tbased models, and a patience of ten epochs was employed. It is\n787\tacknowledged that direct comparison of gradient-descent-based\n788\ttraining with closed-form solutions is not strictly “like-for-like\".\n789\tHowever, all efforts were made to ensure experimental conditions\n790\twere otherwise equivalent. Models wereﬁtted to minimise categorical\n791\tor binary cross-entropy as appropriate. Implementations of Forward-\n792\tForward, Local Supervision, Difference Target Propagation and\n793\tPredictive Coding methods are detailed in supplementary section S.5.\n794\tExplainability analysis\n795\tModel architectures for explainability analysis were equivalent to\n796\tthose used for the main experiments. Here, we\ne x a m i n et h em o r er e s t r i c t i v es e t t i n gw h e r er e t r o g r a d ec o m m u n i c a t i o nf r o m\nneuronal outputs is unavailable for pre-synaptic weight optimisation. We\npropose Forward Projection(FP), a randomised closed-form training method\nrequiring only a single forward pass over the dataset without retrograde\ncommunication. FP generates target values for pre-activation membrane\npotentials through randomised nonlinear projections of pre-synaptic inputs\nand labels. Local loss functions are optimised using closed-form regression\nwithout feedback from downstream layers. A key advantage is interpretability:\nmembrane potentials in FP-trained networks encode information inter-\npretable layer-wise as label predictions. Across several biomedical datasets, FP\nachieves generalisation comparable to gradient descent-based local learning\nmethods while requiring only a single forward propagation step, yielding\nsigniﬁcant training speedup. In few-shot learning tasks, FP produces more\ngeneralisable models than backpropagation-optimised alternatives, with local\ninterpretation functions successfully identifying clinically salient diagnostic\nfeatures.\nA core task in neural network training is synaptic “credit assignment”\nfor error in downstream layers1–3. Although backpropagation has been\nestablished as the standard approach to this problem, its biological\nplausibility has been questioned\n3–5. Backpropagation requires bidir-\nectional synaptic communication, which is incompatible with the\nunidirectional transmission of neural action potentials3. Consequently,\nbackpropagation would require either symmetric neural connectivity\nor a parallel retrograde network for error feedback to earlier layers\n3,6,7.\nThe backward pass traverses layers in reverse order of activation,\nleading to temporal discordance between forward and backward\noperations and necessitating storage of hidden activations. Further-\nmore, gradient descent requires hidden neural activations to be dif-\nferentiable throughout. To approximate backpropagation in a more\nbiologically plausible way, approaches such as Predictive Coding\n5,8 and\nDifference Target Propagation1 seek to align forward neural activity\nwith a backward network that mirrors the forward architecture,\neffectively implementing an inverse model. Various strategies have\nbeen proposed to address the credit assignment problem with\nreduced retrograde communication requirements. Auxiliary loss\nfunctions computed on the activations of individual layers have been\nproposed to shorten the backward pass\n9–12. A prominent example is the\nlayer-wise greedy optimisation method that aims to minimise the costs\nof backpropagation by applying local supervision (LS) at each layer9,13.\nExtensions of this approach include deep continuous local learning10\nand single-layer updating14. Hebbian learning rules using asymmetric\nfeedback weights have been explored, allowing for independent\nupdates for forward and feedback pathways\n15.\nRecent approaches have also explored using two forward passes\nto facilitate communication between upstream and downstream\nneurons\n16–19.T h e “Forward-Forward” (FF) learning algorithm 16 is an\napproach in which data and label hypotheses are combined as inputs,\nwith optimisation seeking to upregulate neural response to correctly\nlabelled inputs and subdue responses to spuriously labelled inputs.\nHowever, inference under the original Forward-Forward algorithm\nReceived: 14 January 2025\nAccepted: 26 January 2026\nCheck for updates\n1Centre for Intelligent Information Processing Systems, Department of Engineering, King’s College London, London, UK. 2Present address: Institute for\nIntelligent Networked Systems, Northeastern University London, E1 8PH London, United Kingdom. e-mail: k1930297@kcl.ac.uk; bipin.rajendran@kcl.ac.uk\nNature Communications|         (2026) 17:2414 1\n1234567890():,;\n1234567890():,;\n\n===== PAGE 2 =====\nrequires a forward pass for each hypothesised label, presenting issues\nfor tasks with large label spaces19.T h e“PEPITA\" algorithm employed a\npreliminary forward pass to predict a label, which is used to generate\nspurious data-label instances for training\n17.\nThe central issue with local learning methods in deep neural\nnetworks is that the optimal activity of hidden neurons is unknown\nduring training, preventing direct observation of local error. Pre-\ndeﬁning activity targets for hidden neurons allows for heuristic local\noptimisation. However, target de ﬁnition strategies are a topic of\nongoing research\n20. Techniques such as local-representation align-\nment and target propagation introduce target values for hidden acti-\nvations using limited retrograde communication from downstream\nneurons\n1,11,20–22. Alternatively, target activities can be set as ﬁxed ran-\ndom label projections computed during the forward pass, thereby\npermitting direct measurement of the error during the forward pass\n11.\nHowever, this approach may lead to highly correlated neuronal activity\n– a problem known as informational collapse 13. Although additive\nnoise permits maximal decorrelation of target potentials 1, it is unin-\nformative with respect to the label, potentially impeding modelﬁtting.\nApproaches such as random neural network features 23 forgo optimi-\nsation of hidden nodes entirely, instead projecting inputs to a random\nhigh-dimensional non-linear feature space. However, random feature\nlayers require exponential scaling with respect to the input dimension\nto support downstream learning\n24.\nThe “Predictive Coding ” paradigm provides a perspective on\nthe collective functionality of intermediate neurons, proposing that\nlocal neural learning processes optimise the prediction of pre-\nsynaptic neural inputs, minimising the “surprise” of out-of-\ndistribution stimuli 5. Approaches such as the “Difference Target\nPropagation”1 reframe network layers as a series of autoencoders,\nwhere intermediate activations represent a series of encodings,\ntransitioning from input information to label information. The issue\nof informational collapse in locally supervised SGD-trained models\nhas been addressed by optimising the retention of information on\nthe pre-synaptic activity\n13. “Prospective con ﬁguration” of target\nactivities reformulates learning under the presumption that idea-\nlised adjustments to neuronal activity should be generated via\nenergy minimisation before synaptic adjustment\n20. Synaptic weights\nmay be modi ﬁed to realise the predetermined activities in response\nto the given input stimuli 20, thereby transitioning from input infor-\nmation to label information through model layers. “Local neural\nsynchronisation\" generates target neural activity for hidden layers\nby projecting neuronal activity onto periodic basis vectors repre-\nsentative of class labels\n25.\nGoing beyond these approaches, we propose the use of random\nnonlinear projections of both pre-synaptic inputs and target labels\nto generate local target activities in the forward pass. The objective\nof this approach is to develop a neural network training algorithm\nthat requires no retrograde communication. Closed-form regression\ntechniques are applicable in this setting, permitting single-step layer\nweight computation without error feedback. Thus, weight para-\nmeters are determined without backward communication from\nneuronal outputs or downstream layers. Further advantages of this\nmethod include the direct interpretability of hidden neurons with\nrespect to local label predictions and stability in the few-shot\nsetting.\nResults\nWe consider a datasetD = fðxi, yiÞgN\ni =1 from the joint distribution (X , Y ).\nThe task is to learn a feed-forward neural network function mapping\nX ! Y .T h em o d e lh a sL layers, with dimensions m0, …, mL,a n da c t i -\nvations a0, …, aL,w h e r ea0 = x and aL = y. Each layer is equipped with\nweightsWl to generate membrane potentialszl = al−1Wl,a n da c t i v a t i o n\nfunctionf l : R ! R to generate neuronal outputsal = fl(zl). The model\nprediction is deﬁned as by = f LðWLð... f 1ðW1xÞÞÞ\n/C1\n.\nForward projection (FP)\nTo generate target membrane potentials for hidden neurons, we pre-\nsent the Forward Projection (FP) algorithm (Fig. 1A). We propose to\ncombine pre-synaptic inputs and labels using random non-linear pro-\njections to generate targets (Fig. 1B). For each training sample, the\ntarget potential ezl 2 R1× ml is generated from pre-synaptic inputs\nal/C0 1 2 R1× ml/C0 1 and labels y 2 R1× mL using ﬁxed random projection\nmatrices Ql 2 Rml/C0 1 × ml and Ul 2 RmL × ml such that\nezl = gl ðal/C0 1Ql Þ + gl ðyUl Þ, ð1Þ\nwhere gl : R ! R is an element-wise non-linear transformation. It is\nnoted here that gl is not necessarily equal to fl, the neural activation\nfunction. Ql and Ul are ﬁxed linear projections drawn from random\nGaussian distributions, which are pre-de ﬁned before training. The\ntarget potential ezl allows a local auxiliary loss function to be de ﬁned\nwith respect to the actual membrane potential,zl, realised during the\nforward pass. The randomised combination of pre-synaptic inputs\nwith the target label is inspired by the high-dimensional computing\nparadigm\n26,w h e r eﬁxed random projections are employed to encode\ninformation from multiple vector inputs, approximately preserving\nrelative distances according to the Johnson-Lindenstrauss lemma\n26.\nAccordingly, it is noted thatezl approximately encodes bothal−1 and y\n(see section S.8 in SI). By generating target potentials in a forward\nmanner, the mix of information encoded in each layer is expected to\ntransition incrementally from predominantly input information in\nearly layers to label information in later layers. We propose to deﬁne a\nlocal loss function L\nl : = k Zl /C0 eZl k, which may be employed as an\nobjective to optimise Wl. Synaptic weights for each layer can be\ncomputed in a closed-form forward manner, using ridge regression\n(Fig. 1C), such that\nW\nl : = ðA>\nl/C0 1Al/C0 1 + λIÞ\n/C0 1\nðA>\nl/C0 1\neZl Þ: ð2Þ\nHere, Al/C0 1 2 RN × ml/C0 1 and eZl 2 RN × ml are matrices of pre-synaptic\nactivities and target potentials, respectively, collected over the N\nsamples in D. λ is a regularisation term, and I is the identity matrix.\nObserve that the A>\nl/C0 1Al/C0 1 and A>\nl/C0 1\neZl terms in ( 2) may be computed\nsequentially over instances fa1, l/C0 1, ... , aN, l/C0 1g/C26 R1× ml/C0 1 ,a n d\nfez1, l , ... , ezN, l g/C26 R1× ml (see section S.7 in SI). Therefore, memory\nrequirements are independent of N, depending only on ml−1 and ml.\nNote, weights are computed once only, after the single training epoch.\nThe approach of encoding information on both inputs and labels in the\nhidden layers is inspired by the Predictive Coding and Target\nPropagation paradigms\n1,5,9. However, unlike previous approaches, no\nbackward communication is required from neuronal outputs to pre-\nsynaptic inputs to achieve the FP ﬁt. As a consequence of this ﬁtting\napproach, neural membrane potentials in hidden layers of FP-trained\nmodels may be interpreted as label predictions (Fig. 1C).\nForward projection performance\nForward Projection was compared to other local learning methods,\nincluding random features (RF) 24, Local Supervision (LS) 9,a n d\nForward-Forward (FF)16 in multiple tasks using equivalent model\narchitectures (Table 1). The Fashion MNIST (FMNIST) image classi ﬁ-\ncation task was modelled on a multi-layer perceptron (MLP). Forward\nProjection achieved higher test accuracy than other local learning\nmethods in this task, approaching the performance of the back-\npropagation reference standard. Two large-scale biomedical sequence\nmodelling tasks were also evaluated with one-dimensional convolu-\ntional neural network (1D-CNN) architectures. The Promoters task\nrequired the identiﬁcation of human non-TATA promoters, a class of\ngene promoter regions that increase transcription of DNA\nsequences\n27,28. Forward projection yielded higher test performance\nthan all other local learning methods in this task. The PTBXL-MI task29\nArticle https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-026-69161-1\nNature Communications|         (2026) 17:2414 2\n\n===== PAGE 3 =====\nrequired diagnosis of myocardial infarction (MI), a heart condition\ncommonly known as “heart attack\", from 12-channel electro-\ncardiogram (ECG) recordings. FP and LS performed comparably in this\ntask. FP also outperformed all other methods in the optimisation of\nmodels with more complex neuronal activations such as modulo and\npolynomial activation functions (see section S.14 in SI). Back-\npropagation performance benchmarks on the PTBXL-MI and Pro-\nmoters tasks were consistent with previous studies\n28,30. For the\nCIFAR10 classi ﬁcation task without data augmentation, two-\ndimensional convolutional neural networks (2D-CNNs) trained by FP\nunderﬁtted, but still outperformed all other local learning methods,\nwhereas standard backpropagation over ﬁtted the model. FP was\nimplemented in a transformer architecture to discriminate the ﬁrst\ntwo classes of the CIFAR10 dataset (\"CIFAR2\"), outperforming RF.\nAlternative feedback-free approaches.T oa s s e s st h ev a l u eo ft h eF P\ntarget generation function, we compared the performance of closed-\nform regression models ﬁtted to targets generated by alternative\nfunctions, including simple label projection ( eZ\nl : = yUl ) and label\nprojection with additive noise eZl : = yUl + E\n/C16/C17\n. To evaluate the cap-\nability of feedback-free training methods to handle information“bot-\ntlenecks\", they were applied to optimise MLP architectures for FMNIST\nclassiﬁcation. MLPs were generated with 1000 hidden neurons in the\nﬁrst and second layers (m\n1 = m2 = 1000), andm3 ∈ {100, 200, 400, 800}\nneurons in theﬁnal hidden layer. FP outperformed other feedback-free\napproaches (Fig. 2A). The performance of RF deteriorated in models\nwith small penultimate layers, as relevant information was less likely to\nbe represented by random projection\n24. The performance of simple\nlabel projection deteriorated in models with large penultimate layers, a\nresult that may be attributable to rank deﬁciency (see section S.6 in SI).\nNoisy label projection maintained steady performance, but at a lower\nlevel than FP.\nFew-shot learning\n\"Few-shot\" learning is a constrained learning scenario in which the\nnumber of data samples available for training is small. High-\ndimensional data, such as images, pose a challenge for few-shot\nl e a r n i n g ,a sm a n ys p u r i o u sf e a t u r e sm a ye x i s t .T h u s ,s u c c e s s f u lf e w -\nshot training methods must select generalisable features in the pre-\nsence of these confounders. Few-shot learning was assessed in image\nclassiﬁcation tasks using a 2D-CNN architecture. The optical coherence\ntomography task (OCT)\n31 required that the model discriminate images\nof healthy retinas from those with choroid neovascularization, a\npathology that affects the eye and manifests mainly as abnormal\ngrowth of blood vessels behind the retina. The paediatric chest X-ray\n(CXR) task\n31 required the model to discriminate between images of\nviral pneumonia, bacterial pneumonia, or healthy controls. The CIFAR2\ntask required the model to discriminate between theﬁrst two CIFAR10\nclasses (aeroplane and automobile). Few-shot training datasets were\ngenerated by subsampling, withN ∈ { 5 ,1 0 ,1 5 ,2 0 ,3 0 ,4 0 ,5 0 }t r a i n i n g\nexamples from each class for OCT and CXR tasks, and\nN ∈ {25, 50, 75, 100} training examples per class for the CIFAR2 task.\nData augmentation was not employed. Model generalisability was\nassessed on all test data (CXR: N\ntest =4 3 1 ;O C T :Ntest = 327; CIFAR2:\nNtest = 2000). In the CIFAR2 few-shot task,\nForward Projection achieved the best performance of any local learn-\ning method in experiments withN ∈ {50, 75, 100}, being outperformed\nonly by backpropagation. With as few as N =1 0t r a i n i n gs a m p l e s ,\nForward Projection ﬁtted discriminative models for the classiﬁcation\nof OCT (test AUC: 84.5 ± 5.8) and CXR (test AUC: 76.6 ± 5.8). CXR and\nOCT datasets highlighted two distinct vulnerabilities of back-\npropagation training in few-shot conditions. In the N = 5 setting on\nOCT, backpropagation over ﬁtted the training samples (Train AUC:\n86.8 ± 13.2; Test AUC: 61.8 ± 17.4), as models integrated noise into\ndecision functions. On the other hand, backpropagation failed to\nachieve adequate modelﬁtting in theN = 10 setting on CXR (Train AUC:\n69.0 ± 8.1; Test AUC: 59.7 ± 6.2). Random features performed com-\nparably to backpropagation in tasks with the fewest training samples,\nbut performance improved only slightly with larger sample sizes.\nRandom Features models could not overﬁt within convolutional layers,\nas these contained no free parameters. However, Random Features\nhad a limited capacity to learn structural features in larger training\nsamples. The improvement that Forward Projection provided\nover Random Features is therefore attributable to structural feature\nlearning within hidden convolutional layers. Label Projection\nand Noisy Label Projection failed to converge in few-shot training\n(training AUC ≈ 0.50). Neither Local Supervision nor Forward-Forward\ntraining matched the baseline generalisability of Random Features\nin OCT and CXR tasks; however, Local Supervision achieved\ncomparable test discrimination to Forward Projection in the CIFAR2\ntask whenN ≥ 75. Predictive coding and Difference Target Propagation\nyielded uninformative models in few-shot learning tasks (Supple-\nmentary Fig. S3). Few-shot performance is tabulated in Supplemental\nTable S2.\nFeature interpretability\nExplainability is a central issue with backpropagation-based learning,\nas relationships between hidden activations and model predictions\nmay be non-monotonic, complicating the interpretation of hidden\nneural activities\n32. An important advantage of Forward Projection is the\ninterpretability of hidden neuron activity with respect to label pre-\ndictions (see section S.8 in SI). In the\nFMNIST task, test accuracy of layer explanations was observed to\nimprove between early layers and subsequent layers (Supplementary\nFig. S2-A), demonstrating progressive learning. In the PTBXL-MI task,\napplying the surrogate layer explanation function (described in\nequation (3)) to the convolutional layers identi ﬁed various clinically\nsalient features for diagnosing myocardial infarction (MI). Consequently, the model must learn several distinct\npathological waveform features, including elevation of the “ST\"\nsegment, or inversion of the “T\" wave. The training\nprocedure for each layer is presented in Supplementary Fig. S1.\nThe memory requirement for Forward Projection isOðm2Þ for the\nlayer weights,Oðm2Þ for the Q matrix, andOðmmLÞ for the U matrix. As\nthe A>\nl/C0 1Al/C0 1 and A>\nl/C0 1\neZl terms in (2) can be accumulated sequentially\nover data batches (See section S.7 in SI), twom × m matrices sufﬁce for\ntheir storage, thereby avoiding storage of theN × m matrices Al−1 and\neZl . As with all methods, Forward Projection requires Oðm2Þ multiply-\nand-accumulate (MAC) operations to calculate the activations for the\ndownstream layer in the forward pass. To generate target potentials\nduring the forward pass, Forward Projection also requires Oðm\n2Þ\nadditional MAC operations to project through Q and OðmmLÞ MAC\noperations to project through U. Computation of the A>\nl/C0 1Al/C0 1 and\nA>\nl/C0 1\neZl terms requires OðNm2Þ MAC operations each. After all samples\nhave been observed, computation of model weights using equation (2)\nrequires Oðm3Þ operations to invert the A>\nl/C0 1Al/C0 1 term and Oðm3Þ\noperations for matrix multiplication to complete the regression;\nhowever, this happens only once for each layer. Furthermore, Forward\nProjection requires no backward pass.\nFor backpropagation,Oðm\n2Þ memory is required for storing layer\nweights, Oðm2Þ for accumulated gradients, and OðmÞ for activations.\nFP\nLP\nLPN\nRF\n0.75\n0.78\n0.81\n0.84\n200 400 600 800\nHidden Layer Dimension\nTest Accuracy\nA\nBP\nFF\nFP\nLS\nRF\n0.5\n0.6\n0.7\n0.8\n10 20 30 40 50\nN Train\nTest AUC\nB\nBP\nFF\nFP\nLS\nRF\n0.5\n0.6\n0.7\n0.8\n0.9\n10 20 30 40 50\nN Train\nTest AUC\nC BP\nFF\nFP\nLS\nRF\n0.6\n0.7\n0.8\n0.9\n25 50 75 100\nN Train\nTest AUC\nD\nFig. 2 | Performance of Forward Projection with backpropagation and local\nlearning approaches. Notably, compute scales as Oðm3Þ for FP versus\nOðNem2Þ for BP. For example, training a dense hidden layer with 1000\ninputs and 1000 outputs on the FMNIST dataset (N = 60, 000, mL =1 0 )\nby Forward Projection requires a total of 1.2 × 1011 MAC operations for\nthe forward pass and 1.2 × 1011 MAC operations for the weight update.\nTraining the same layer via 100 epochs of backpropagation requires\n6.0 × 10\n12 MAC operations for forward passes and 1.8 × 1013 operations\nfor weight updates. Forward projection requires only a single training\nepoch, reducing overall computation time accordingly. Note that Local\nSupervision and Forward-Forward algorithms have similar computa-\ntional and memory requirements as backpropagation (Table 2 and\nTable S3). Training complexities of other methods are discussed in\ns e c t i o nS . 1 3i nS I .\nDiscussion\nWe present the Forward Projection algorithm, which enables learning\nin a single pass over the dataset using random projections and closed-\nform optimisation. Compared to state-of-the-art local learning meth-\nods that require observing post-synaptic neuronal outputs to optimise\nsynaptic weights via error-based gradient descent, Forward Projection\noperates under a stricter constraint,ﬁtting weights using just the pre-\nsynaptic neuronal activity and labels.\nThe target generation function proposed here for Forward Pro-\njection promotes input and label encoding in neural membrane\npotentials (see section S.8 in SI). Joint encoding of labels with pre-\nsynaptic activity alleviates the degenerate neural activity which results\nfrom local modelling of simple label projections, whilst avoiding the\nintroduction of uninformative noise (see section S.6 in SI). In this\nanalysis, a simple non-linearity was employed for target generation.\nThe utility of more complex nonlinearities for target generation is a\nsubject for further research.\nExplainability of neural network models is an important limitation\nin decision-critical ﬁelds such as biomedicine, where errors such as\nconfounded decisions may lead to signi ﬁcant consequences\n35.F P -\ntrained layers may be interpreted without downstream information,\nproviding insight into model reasoning in hidden layers. FP inter-\npretation yielded informative outputs in three model architectures,\nidentifying clinically salient features in ECG sequences and OCT ima-\nges. An important advantage of FP training is that saliency maps may\nbe generated before downstream ﬁtting, permitting on-the- ﬂy\ninspection of intermediate layers for suf ﬁciency or confounding.\nThus, expert scrutiny of hidden layer performance may be conducted\neven before the downstream architecture isﬁnalised.\nFew-shot learning is ubiquitous in biological systems, which\nexhibit rapid neuronal adaptation to changing environments\n36.I no u r\nexperiments, Forward Projection demonstrated clear performance\nadvantages over backpropagation and local learning approaches in\nfew-shot learning tasks, presenting a plausible method for learning\nnew tasks rapidly. In this setting, convolutional features learned by\nForward Projection yielded more generalisable models than back-\npropagation, which overﬁtted in some experiments and underﬁtted in\nothers, even underperforming random features in some cases. For-\nward Projection also maintained reasonable performance on activa-\ntion functions that were untrainable by SGD-based methods (see\ns e c t i o nS . 1 4i nS I ) .\nThe closed-form Forward Projectionﬁti sc o m p u t a b l ei nas i n g l e\npass over the data for each layer, presenting an opportunity to\nexpedite training and reduce environmental footprint. The efﬁciency\nof FP training is attributable to a substantially different operational\nsequence from that employed in SGD-based approaches. Firstly, FP\ntraining collects a Gram matrix of pre-synaptic activity over a single\nepoch, which is subsequently inverted during a one-step weight matrix\ncomputation. Secondly, FP completes ﬁtting for each layer before\ninitialising successive layers. In contrast, SGD-based approachesﬁta l l\nthe layers in the network iteratively, using feedback from neuronal\noutputs and downstream layers. FP training requires no retrograde\ncommunication between neural output activations and presynaptic\nconnections, enabling direct training on hardware with unidirectional\nsynaptic and neuronal communication. This differs from current\niterative local learning methods, which require backward commu-\nnication from neuronal outputs to optimise pre-synaptic parameters\n(see section S.2 in SI). Although FP addresses the feedback-free con-\nstraint inherent in biological learning systems, the biological plausi-\nbility of one-step learning via matrix inversion remains uncertain.\nIterative ﬁtting via gradient descent in sequential, batch-based learn-\ning offers a practical and scalable approach for aligning weights with\nlocally generated targets, as de ﬁned in ( 1) (see section S.11 in SI).\nAlthough closed-form optimisation remains a challenge for recurrent\narchitectures, future research will explore integrating iterative training\nwith random temporal convolutions to generalise the FP framework to\ndynamic models and expand its applicability to temporal learning\ntasks. This backpropagation-free strategy holds promise for biologi-\ncally inspired computing systems, such as spiking neural networks,\nwhere non-differentiable activation functions preclude standard\nbackpropagation\n37–39. FP training may be applicable as a pre-training\nstep to reduce the number of training epochs required for back-\npropagation. In conclusion, FP is an ef ﬁcient approach for neural\nnetwork optimisation, employing techniques from randomised pro-\njective embedding and linear regression to ﬁtw e i g h tm a t r i c e si no n e\nepoch using a single-step solution. Interpretability of hidden neurons\nin FP-trained models may be employed to improve the explainability of\nneural network predictions.\nMethods\nGeneralisability of machine learning methods to real-world datasets\nrequires robustness to adverse modelling conditions such as class\nimbalance and noise, which often impede performance\n40.T oa s s e s st h e\napplicability and generalisability of Forward Projection and local\nlearning methods in diverse real-world conditions, performance was\nevaluated in benchmark tasks from four biomedical domains\ndescribed below.\nPTBXL-MI\nThe PTB-XL dataset contains 12-lead electrocardiography (ECG)\nrecordings from 18,889 participants\n29,41. ECG recordings of ten-second\nduration and 100Hz sample rate were used in our experiments.
